"end_unit": 9, "locator": "extracted HTML block 7 through extracted HTML block 9", "exact_excerpt": "ABC Electoral Infografías\nABC Electoral TV\nABC Electoral Radio\nAlerta INE sobre inconstitucionalidades y consecuencias indeseables de la reforma en las elecciones\nPublicado el:\n25 de enero de 2023\nCategoría:\nAl día\n,\nDestacada INE\n,\nNoticias\nEscrito por:\nINE\nTema:\nComunicados\nAlerta INE sobre inconstitucionalidades y consecuencias indeseables de la reforma en las elecciones\nPublicado el:\nenero 25, 2023\nCategoría:\nAl día\n,\nDestacada INE\n,\nNoticias\n\nESCRITO POR:\nINE\nTEMA:\n[primer_tag]\nNúmero: 013\nInstruyen consejeras y consejeros al Secretario Ejecutivo a presentar los recursos legales para contrarrestar la reforma electoral\nEstamos ante la primera reforma regresiva de nuestra democracia: Lorenzo Córdova\nEl Consejo General del Instituto Nacional Electoral (INE) conoció el Informe sobre la incidencia en la función electoral de los decretos de reforma que modifican las leyes generales de Comunicación Social, de Responsabilidades Administrativas, que fueron publicadas el 27 de diciembre de 2022, así como de Instituciones y Procedimientos Electorales, de Partidos Políticos, la Ley Orgánica del Poder Judicial de la Federación y la Ley General de los Medios de Impugnación en Materia Electoral, cuyo proceso legislativo continuará a partir del 1 de febrero, y que son conocidas por la opinión pública como “Plan B”.\nEl documento analiza y explica las implicaciones que tendrían estas reformas en la celebración de elecciones libres y auténticas, como lo mandata la Constitución, y las afectaciones en materia de autonomía del Instituto Nacional Electoral, la estructura territorial de los órganos ejecutivos del INE en los 300 distritos electorales y en las 32 entidades federativas, los procedimientos de capacitación y organización electoral, los avances para combatir la violencia política contra las mujeres en razón de género y las acciones afirmativas para potenciar la representación política de núcleos de la población tradicionalmente desfavorecidos.\nAdemás se señalan consecuencias en materia de fiscalización de los ingresos y gastos de los actores políticos, el cumplimiento de las obligaciones de los partidos políticos, la protección de los datos personales de la ciudadanía, los derechos laborales de quienes integran el Servicio Profesional Electoral Nacional y la Rama Administrativa de los Organismos Públicos Locales, así como el respeto al pacto federal y la libre configuración legislativa de las entidades federativas que se ven afectados en diversa graduación por las reformas electorales.\nEl Informe tiene como objetivo poner a disposición de la ciudadanía la opinión especializada del órgano del Estado mexicano encargado, junto con los Organismos Públicos Locales, de organizar los comicios para la renovación de las instancias públicas mediante el sufragio, sobre los riesgos que podrían ocasionarse con algunas de las reformas y, especialmente, para el ejercicio, garantía y defensa de derechos político-electorales fundamentales de las ciudadanas y ciudadanos mexicanos.\nEl Consejero Presidente del INE, Lorenzo Córdova Vianello, afirmó que si el proceso legislativo de la reforma legal se culmina y se publican los cambios legales, “lo que tendremos delante de nosotros es la primera reforma regresiva de nuestra democracia y estaremos en la antesala de una inédita incertidumbre preelectoral y postelectoral en la que será, en 2024, la elección más grande de nuestra historia”.\n“La reforma afecta gravemente las posibilidades del ejercicio del voto ciudadano en condiciones de libertad y equidad, con regularidad y certeza. Es decir, impacta en la recreación misma de nuestra democracia, tal como se ha construido, colectivamente, a lo largo de tres décadas por varias generaciones de mexicanas y mexicanos”, indicó Córdova Vianello.\nEn este sentido, el Consejero Presidente apeló a la responsabilidad con la República y con la estabilidad política del país de quienes tendrán que resolver sobre la constitucionalidad y legalidad de las reformas del “Plan B”. “Que detengan este auténtico desmantelamiento de nuestra democracia”.\nEl INE, recalcó, acudirá a todas las instancias legales “para defender la democracia, garantizar el derecho de toda la ciudadanía, incluidas minorías y también, por supuesto, mayorías a participar en el futuro del país y el derecho de cada ciudadana y ciudadano a ejercer el voto libre y que éste sea bien contado y cuente para la conformación democrática del poder político en México”.\nReforma implica retroceso en materia de igualdad de género y no discriminación\nEn su intervención, la Consejera Norma De la Cruz advirtió dos afectaciones en materia de igualdad de género y no discriminación: la disminución de 50 a 25 fórmulas de candidaturas que incluyen personas indígenas, migrantes, residentes en el extranjero, con discapacidad, afromexicanas y de la diversidad sexual, así como la reestructura orgánica del Instituto contemplada, que implicaría no contar con una Unidad Técnica de Igualdad de Género y No Discriminación, “al proponer que sus funciones sean absorbidas por una nueva dirección ejecutiva de partidos políticos”.\nComo presidenta de la Comisión de Igualdad de Género y No Discriminación, enfatizó que, de ser aprobada en sus términos, ésta sería una medida regresiva, pues la existencia de una unidad técnica especializada sirve para garantizar “la transversalidad de la perspectiva de género en todas y cada una de las decisiones, acuerdos, lineamientos, reglamentos, resoluciones, procedimientos y acciones que implementa esta autoridad administrativa”.\nEn materia de fiscalización se abre la oportunidad a la simulación y ocultamiento de operaciones\nPor su parte, el Consejero Jaime Rivera destacó las afectaciones que la reforma conlleva en materia de fiscalización al restar autonomía técnica constitucional a la Unidad Técnica de Fiscalización al convertirla en una Dirección Ejecutiva. Además de restringir la posibilidad de fiscalizar actividades irregulares que los sujetos realicen o que no estén previstos en los supuestos establecidos en la ley, las modificaciones legales impiden que el INE cancele candidaturas a quienes no cumplen con transparentar sus ingresos y gastos, mencionó.\n“La reforma propone que la violación de no entregar informes de ingresos y gastos de precampaña deje de ser sancionada de manera severa y establece una ampliación de los plazos en los cuales los partidos están obligados a reportar en el Sistema Integral de Fiscalización las operaciones de ingresos y gastos, lo que abre la oportunidad de simulaciones y ocultamientos de operaciones”, dijo Rivera.\nAfectaciones en materia de organización electoral\nLa presidenta de la Comisión de Organización Electoral, Carla Humphrey dio cuenta de las afectaciones que tendrá la reestructura organizacional y la eliminación de áreas fundamentales, “al dejar inadvertida la especialización y el profesionalismo de cada una, sin analizar objetiva y exhaustivamente sus consecuencias”.\nExpuso que las actividades de la Dirección de Organización y de la Dirección de Capacitación representan más del 40% del total de actividades institucionales que se desarrollan a lo largo del proceso electoral y al desaparecer las juntas distritales ejecutivas se prescindiría del personal de la rama administrativa que equivale al menos al 89% de estas direcciones; ello, aunado a la fusión de tres unidades responsables pondría en riesgo los estándares de calidad de los procesos electorales.\nReforma afecta esquema de integración de casillas\n\nEl Consejero Martín Faz consideró que los impactos de la reforma en materia de capacitación electoral son particularmente graves, “ya que modifican sensiblemente el esquema con que hemos operado la integración de las Mesas Directivas de Casilla, tanto en las actividades específicas, como en la capacidad operativa de quienes las realizan y coordinan”.\nEntre las principales afectaciones de la reforma, el presidente de la Comisión de Capacitación Electoral y Educación Cívica del INE refirió que se reduce el número de personas sorteadas o insaculadas, al pasar del 13% al 10% de cada sección electoral, lo que traerá como consecuencia “que en un importante número de secciones principalmente urbanas puedan llegar a no tener a la ciudadanía suficiente que cumpla con los requisitos para integrar las casillas”.\nEn riesgo Padrón Electoral confiable\nEl Consejero Ciro Murayama subrayó que la reforma electoral echa a la calle a quienes coordinan el trabajo de los módulos, desde donde se actualizan el Padrón y la cartografía electoral. “Afectar la actualización del Padrón es dañar de base el trabajo de planeación, organización y ejecución de las elecciones auténticas que México ya tiene, pero que se quieren dañar de forma irresponsable”.\nAdemás, advirtió que las modificaciones legales establecen que los módulos del INE se muevan preferentemente a espacios como oficinas gubernamentales, donde no se tiene certeza que se resguarden los datos sensibles de la ciudadanía.\nSe limita función de los OPL\nLa Consejera Dania Ravel, presidenta de la Comisión de Vinculación, externó su preocupación por que la reforma limita el funcionamiento de los Organismos Públicos Locales al fusionar todas las áreas relevantes en dos mega-áreas: una de organización, capacitación electoral y educación cívica y otra de administración, prerrogativas y asuntos jurídicos.\n“Que desde la Ley General de Instituciones y Procedimientos Electorales se establezca la estructura de los órganos electorales autónomos, conlleva a la inaplicación de las leyes expedidas por los congresos locales en ejercicio de su soberanía y se desconocen las particularidades de cada una de las entidades que componen nuestro país”.\nFusión de Dirección Jurídica y Unidad de lo Contencioso Electoral no es funcional\nPor su parte, la Consejera Adriana Favela, presidenta de la Comisión de Quejas y Denuncias, advirtió que la fusión de la Dirección Jurídica y la Unidad Técnica de lo Contencioso Electoral, propuesta en la reforma, puede generar una excesiva carga de trabajo.\n“No es factible que este órgano técnico especializado se vuelva a integrar a la Dirección Jurídica porque no sería posible hacer el trabajo tan especializado y con tanta calidad”, como el que lleva a cabo la Unidad Técnica de lo Contencioso Electoral, expresó. Además, recordó que los órganos distritales coadyuvan en la recepción de las quejas, en la investigación y revisión de temas de fiscalización, por lo que son fundamentales en esta materia.\nLa credencial de elector es la llave que abre el derecho a votar desde nuestro sistema electoral\nLa Consejera Claudia Zavala especificó que la reforma permite la construcción del Padrón y el listado nominal de personas que residen en el extranjero a partir de dos documentos que son ajenos a la naturaleza electoral y que rompe con toda la trazabilidad electoral y la certeza que requieren en su integración.\n“Esto rompe con el postulado constitucional que otorga al INE todas estas garantías y comisiones de vigilancia que se construye desde la parte colaborativa con los partidos políticos”, refirió, pues quebranta garantías de control para el ejercicio del voto como es la credencial de elector. Nadie que carezca de credencial de elector en México podría ejercer su derecho porque “es la llave que abre el derecho desde nuestro sistema electoral”.\nZavala añadió que “una reforma que pone en peligro a la democracia, la República y los derechos humanos debe ser cuestionada”.\nReforma pudo haber centrado esfuerzos en otras áreas de oportunidad\nEl Consejero José Roberto Ruiz cuestionó que habiendo grandes áreas de oportunidad para disminuir el costo del sistema electoral, éstas no se abordan en las iniciativas de las representaciones políticas.\nInquirió por qué no se destinaron esfuerzos para tener un calendario electoral único en el país, ya que a pesar de los avances obtenidos aún no se completa la unificación de los mismos, por lo que no se ven estrategias que vayan a impactar a esa reducción del sistema electoral, o por qué no se buscó prescindir del doble financiamiento público que tienen los partidos nacionales, al recibir casi 6 mil millones de pesos a nivel nacional y una cifra similar en lo local por las 32 entidades federativas.\nAusteridad pasa por el atropello de los derechos laborales\nLa reforma electoral no genera un avance democrático, ni garantiza mejora en los derechos de la ciudadanía, sostuvo el Consejero Uuc-kib Espadas, quien aseveró que la mutilación de la estructura del INE que se plantea atropella derechos humanos y laborales de, probablemente, miles de trabajadores.\n“No podemos simplemente aceptar que la austeridad pase por el atropello de los derechos laborales, por lastimar a las miles de familias cuyo legítimo sustento depende del trabajo profesional de los integrante del Servicio Profesional Electoral”, estableció.\nEl INE no regatea a las instancias legislativas el ejercicio de sus atribuciones\nEl presentar el informe, el Secretario Ejecutivo del INE, Edmundo Jacobo, hizo hincapié en el reconocimiento que tiene la autoridad electoral sobre las facultades del Poder Legislativo; sin embargo, -agregó- las propuestas de modificaciones legales han sido confeccionadas sin tomar en consideración la experiencia y conocimiento técnico de la autoridad electoral, pese a que, el Instituto ha mantenido siempre una posición de colaboración con todas las instituciones del Estado mexicano.\n“En democracia, me parece, no cabe regatearles a las instancias legislativas el ejercicio de sus atribuciones como representantes populares. Ahí radica la esencia del fenómeno democrático: en encauzar las inquietudes, aspiraciones y preocupaciones de la ciudadanía”, dijo, y argumentó que la construcción de diagnósticos, que tomen en cuenta la experiencia y conocimiento acumulados de las instancias técnicas electorales, es condición esencial para un mejor diseño de los cambios necesarios y pertinentes a las reglas del juego democrático.\nDurante la sesión, las y los consejeros electorales instruyeron al Secretario Ejecutivo del INE, a interponer todos los recursos jurídicos y legales a su alcance para combatir esta reforma electoral que afecta gravemente a la democracia del país.", "excerpt_sha256": "2541648fe67fcf6a5bf09c21d613367843d876bacaf543e4402f84f875df466a", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E008", "source_id": "SRC-42b417feb578efb9", "url": "https://www.amnesty.org/en/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/AMR4169922023ENGLISH.pdf", "title": "Mexico: Rushing Headfirst into an Abyss of Human Rights?", "publisher": "Amnesty International", "raw_sha256": "4c08bee968bc380d9e094f716f011cd624df9dcc1f581044778fffa78bf7ba4a", "fact_summary": "The report stated that the president publicly attacked the Supreme Court after it blocked his attempt to place the National Guard under military control, and that he accused Indigenous and environmental defenders concerned about the Mayan Train of being “false environmentalists.” It also reported stigmatization, repression, disproportionate or unnecessary force, arbitrary arrests, sexual violence, criminalization of protest and impunity in the situations discussed.", "question_ids": ["3B.3", "3B.4", "3B.8", "4B.3", "4B.4", "7B.6"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The report documents conduct and institutional conditions relevant to 2023, including the Supreme Court’s April 2023 ruling.", "ruler_attribution": "The public statements and political attacks were attributed directly to López Obrador; other abuses involved state and local authorities.", "limitations": ["The source does not establish that every protest-related abuse was personally directed by the president."], "start_unit": 3, "end_unit": 5, "locator": "page 3 through page 5", "exact_excerpt": "10. Despite the militarization of public security defended and promoted by the President and furthered by Congress,\n         the National Human Rights Commission has not questioned these decisions, neither publicly nor in the courts. This\n         has been considered by local organizations as a lack of impartiality. 19 Speedy implementation of supported\n         recommendations from both the second 20 and third reviews21 to strengthen the work of the NCHR would help\n         address this concern.\n\n     11. When ruling against the president’s initiatives, the Supreme Court has received consistent public attacks by the\n         president that compromise the autonomy of the judiciary. For example, when the Supreme Court blocked the\n         president’s attempt to place the National Guard under the control of the military, the president accused the judges\n         of “responding to the interests of the elites and not to the voice of people”. 22 Speedy implementation of supported\n         recommendations would help to preserve the independence of the judiciary and guarantee the rights to truth and\n         justice.23\n\n     12. In January 2021, Mexico ratified the Escazú Agreement, which includes strong protections for Indigenous and\n         environmental defenders.24 However, the President responded by publicly accusing the human rights defenders\n         who have expressed concern about the Mayan Train Project of being “false environmentalists”.25 Six UN Special\n         Procedure mandate holders expressed concerns about that project, relating to Indigenous Peoples´ rights to land,\n         health, and environmental impacts.26 This replicates concerns raised in Mexico´s last UPR.27\n\n     13. Arraigo has not been repealed despite the fact that the IACHR ordered Mexico to eliminate it in 2022 28 and again\n         in 2023.29 The IACHR also stated that legal and constitutional provisions must be amended to make the mandatory\n         pre-trial detention compatible with the American Convention on Human Rights.\n\n\n\nTHE HUMAN RIGHTS SITUATION ON THE GROUND\nVIOLENCE AGAINST WOMEN AND GIRLS: FEMINICIDES\n\n     14. Instances of gender-based violence against women and girls have continued to rise since the last review in 2018.\n         According to official data from the Executive Secretariat of the National Public Security System, 30 between 2018\n         and May 2023, at least 20,292 women were killed. This implies that almost 11 women are killed daily in Mexico.\n         The prosecutors’ offices decided to investigate 5,065 of these killings as alleged feminicides.\n\n     15. Since 2015 the “Gender-based Violence against Women Alert” (AVGM) 31 has been active in Mexico. It relies on\n         coordinated efforts to prevent and address/respond to gender-based violence against women. Despite the activation\n         of 25 AVGMs in 22 states of the country, impunity in the investigations of feminicides remain unabated.32\n\n     16. In 2021, Amnesty International documented four emblematic cases of feminicides. 33 The report shows\n         shortcomings in the investigations such as loss of evidence, lack of proper investigations, failure in applying gender\n         perspective, and threats to and revictimization of the victim’s families. These patterns are similar to those found in\n         Ciudad Juarez’s feminicides investigations, as has been repeatedly highlighted by NGOs and rights holders.34\n\n\nSOCIAL PROTEST\n\n     17. People exercising their right to peacefully protest often face violations of their rights, including stigmatization,\n         repression, unnecessary or disproportionate use of force, threats, digital harassment, lack of due process and\n         access to justice, impunity, and sexual violence in case of women.\n\n     18. Stigmatization often comes from the highest authorities, including the president’s daily morning press conference,\n         where he often expresses strong criticism of journalists, human rights defenders and NGOs that question\n         government actions, accusing them of being “conservatives”, “opponents” as well as of resorting to “hammers,\n         torches and Molotov cocktails” when participating in protests. 35\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                   INDEX AMR 41/6992/2023\n MEXICO: RUSHING HEADFIRST INTO AN ABYSS OF HUMAN RIGHTS?                                          JULY 2023\n Submission to the 45th session of the UPR Working Group, January-February                         LANGUAGE: ENGLISH\n 2024                                                                        2\n                                                                                                   amnesty.org\n\n19. Amnesty´s report The Age of Women: Stigma and violence against women protesters 36 documented human rights\n         violations committed bylaw enforcement officials against women demanding a life free from violence in Sinaloa, the\n         State of Mexico, Mexico City, Guanajuato, and Quintana Roo in 2020. These violations include excessive and\n         unnecessary use of force, illegal and arbitrary arrests, gender-based verbal and physical abuse, and sexual\n         violence, stigmatization of feminist demonstrations as “violent” as well as discriminatory bias against women\n         covering their faces. Currently, no one has been held to account for these abuses.\n\n     20. The criminal justice system is often used as a deterrence mechanism for those protesting especially for land,\n         territorial, environmental and climate rights. Since the last review, Amnesty has documented cases of the\n         criminalization of protest.37 These cases show a tendency to criminalize leaders and human rights defenders by\n         using vague or broad criminal offences or fabricating criminal charges against them. Violations of rights in these\n         cases include lack of due process and access to justice, prolonged trial processes with the constant threat of\n         deprivation of liberty, as well as lack of prior informed consent in the cases of indigenous communities, and\n         impunity.\n\n\nSAFETY OF JOURNALISTS\n\n     21. Mexico is one of the most dangerous countries in the world for journalists. 2020 38 and 202239 were some of the\n         deadliest years in Mexican history for the press, with at least 19 and 13 journalists killed respectively because of\n         their work, more than in 202140 and 2019.41\n\n     22. At the federal level, since 2012, Mexico has had a Mechanism for the Protection of Human Rights Defenders and\n         Journalists (“the Mechanism”),42 which provides certain protection measures for people who have received attacks\n         or threats. Currently, the Mechanism benefits more than 500 journalists,43 but from 2017 to 2021, at least seven\n         journalists have been killed despite having been afforded protection by the Mechanism. 44\n\n     23. Furthermore, many cases remain without adequate investigation and the Mechanism continued to fall short of its\n         goal to safeguard the lives and safety of these groups.45 The Mechanism has not yet provided a strategy to respond\n         to digital attacks and unlawful surveillance, such as from the NSO Group’s spyware Pegasus. 46\n\nMILITARIZATION\n\n     24. Mexico has relied on a heavily militarized public security strategy. A constitutional amendment in 2019 47 created\n         the National Guard (GN), which was intended to be under civilian control. However, the GN is heavily influenced\n         by the Armed Forces (AF); a former army general is in command, and most of its members were formerly part of\n         the AF. The fifth transitory article in the constitutional reforms provided that the AF would participate in public\n         security tasks for up to five years while the GN becomes fully operational.48\n\n     25. In 2020, an executive decree formally involved the AF in public security tasks until 2024, 49 a period that was\n         recently extended after the Congress agreed to keep the army on the streets until 2028, 50 without a strategy for\n         demilitarization of public security and law enforcement agencies and police reform. In November 2022, the\n         Supreme Court resolved that the Presidential Decree of 2020 allowing the permanent participation of AF in public\n         security operations until 2024 was constitutional. 51\n\n     26. In September 2022, Congress approved an initiative that places the GN formally under the control of Mexico´s\n         Ministry of National Defence (SEDENA). 52 However, on 18 April 2023, the Supreme Court struck down the transfer\n         of the GN to SEDENA.53\n\n     27. The GN is among the ten federal institutions with the highest number of complaints for human rights violations.\n         The NHRC has received 1,200 complaints against the GN, since its creation in 2019 up to September 2022. 54\n         These complaints related to arbitrary detentions, torture and other ill-treatment, enforced disappearances and other\n         violations. As for SEDENA and SEMAR (Secretariat of the Navy), from 2019 to September 2022, the NHRC collected\n         1,604 complaints and 1,553 respectively, regarding multiple violations of human rights, including torture and other\n         ill-treatment, unlawful killings, enforced disappearances and arbitrary detentions. 55\n\n\n\n                                                                                                 INDEX AMR 41/6992/2023\n MEXICO: RUSHING HEADFIRST INTO AN ABYSS OF HUMAN RIGHTS?                                        JULY 2023\n Submission to the 45th session of the UPR Working Group, January-February                       LANGUAGE: ENGLISH\n 2024                                                                        3\n                                                                                                 amnesty.org\n\n28. Participation of the AF in the public life of Mexico has increased the probability of being a victim of sexual torture\n         when detained; led to more violations of judicial guarantees during detention; and fostered a lack of truth,\n         transparency and accountability in its operations.56\n\n     29. In addition, military personnel have been directly linked to the enforced disappearance of the 43 Ayotzinapa\n         students, extrajudicial executions and cases of torture and other ill-treatment.57\n\n\n\nRECOMMENDATIONS FOR ACTION BY THE STATE UNDER REVIEW\nAmnesty International calls on the government of Mexico to:\n\nNational human rights framework\n     30. As Mexico previously agreed to do, strengthen the National Human Rights Commission so that it complies with the\n         Paris Principles and ensure that its recommendations are in line with international human rights norms and\n         standards, including consideration of the needs of victims as well as of standards on comprehensive reparations.\n\n     31. Ensure that law enforcement personnel and those working in the criminal justice system are provided with adequate\n         resources to carry out their functions efficiently and in accordance with international human rights standards,\n         including decent and appropriate compensation and working conditions.\n\nViolence against women and girls\n     32. Publicly acknowledge the scale of the problem of feminicides and disappearances in Mexico, as well as the failings\n         in the investigations into these crimes, and send a clear message that these crimes will no longer be tolerated or\n         go unpunished.\n\n     33. Guarantee full reparations family members of feminicide victims in line with the “Basic Principles and Guidelines\n         on the Right to a Remedy and Reparation for Victims of Gross Violations of International Human Rights Law and\n         Serious Violations of International Humanitarian Law” and the CEDAW General Recommendation 35.\n\n     34. Investigate the problem of failures in criminal investigations of crimes of violence against women, especially\n         feminicides and disappearances and issue recommendations that address the aspects highlighted in this report,\n         such as excessive workloads, failure to investigate, the application of a gender perspective, the collection and\n         safeguarding of evidence and the need for training of public servants in Public Prosecutors’ Offices.\n\n\nSocial Protest\n   35. Publicly recognize the legitimacy of the right to peaceful protest, facilitate and protect persons exercising this right,\n        and abstain from their stigmatization and criminalization.\n\n     36. Carry out prompt, thorough, independent and impartial investigations, with a gender and intersectional perspective,\n         into violations of human rights occurring before, during and after protests.\n\n     37. Guarantee access to justice and full reparation for victims of human rights violations in the context of protests in\n         line with the “Basic Principles and Guidelines on the Right to a Remedy and Reparation for Victims of Gross\n         Violations of International Human Rights Law and Serious Violations of International Humanitarian Law”.\n\n     38. Amend the National Law on the Use of Force (specifically Articles 27, 28 and 36) in line with international human\n         rights law, the “Basic Principles on the Use of Force and Firearms by Law Enforcement Officials”, the UN Code of\n         Conduct for Law Enforcement Officials, and in accordance with relevant judgments issued by the IACHR.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                   INDEX AMR 41/6992/2023\n MEXICO: RUSHING HEADFIRST INTO AN ABYSS OF HUMAN RIGHTS? The Supreme Court later invalidated important parts of Plan B, suspended other provisions and preserved institutional checks, while the INE conducted successful local elections in June 2023.", "question_ids": ["4B.1", "4B.10", "4B.2", "4B.3", "4B.4", "4B.6", "4B.9", "8B.2", "8B.8", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly relevant to 2023, including the Senate’s February approval and court decisions through June 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The reforms, budget proposals and attacks were attributed to López Obrador; the constraints came from Congress, courts and civil society.", "limitations": ["The report uses strong analytical language and incorporates cited commentary from other observers."], "start_unit": 26, "end_unit": 28, "locator": "page 26 through page 28", "exact_excerpt": "Politics\nThe election of López Obrador in 2018 revealed a country whose citizens are highly polarized and\ndominated by strong feelings of disillusionment with the political establishment. Building an anti-\ncorruption, anti-neoliberal economic discourse while avoiding firm positions on controversial issues\nsuch as gay marriage or abortion rights, AMLO managed to appeal to voters across a wide range of political\norientations.110 Once in power, however, he used the excuse of combating corruption to justify his need to\nfully command the government and wield more power than his predecessors.111 Who will occupy the Palacio\nNacional in 2024 remains an open question, but regardless of the outcome, AMLO’s attacks on democratic\ninstitutions, the dynamics of the Morena party and the opposition, and U.S.-Mexico relations will heavily shape\nboth the campaign and post-campaign environments.\nAttacks against Democracy\nAMLO’s style has steadily become more populist, and his attacks against independent democratic institutions built\nover the past 30 years have led Mexico down a perilous route. Especially concerning has been his weaponization of\nanti-corruption rhetoric in Mexico, the crutch that supports many of his most controversial political projects.\nHis strategy to silence critics and avoid checks and balances has broken institutions, cutting their budgets and\nplacing loyalists in key positions of power. The INE, the Federal Economic Competition Commission (COFECE),\nand the INAI have all seen their budgets slashed.112 The proposal for the 2023 budget published in November 2022\nfit this trend. It cut the INE’s budget by nearly 4.5 billion pesos, the INAI’s by 18.6 million, and the COFECE’s by\n33.5 million.113 In the case of the National Human Rights Commission (CNDH), the controversial election of Rosario\nPiedra Ibarra, who openly supported AMLO’s political projects, as its head raised concerns over the extent to which\nthe commission could remain silent over human rights violations committed by the military and simultaneously\n22 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\noperate independently from the government.114 Piedra spoke in favor of AMLO’s electoral reform, explicitly showing\nhow the commission is politically aligned with AMLO and how its autonomy has been compromised.115\nAlong the same lines, AMLO has denied human rights abuses and has directly attacked nongovernmental\norganizations, human rights defenders, women’s organizations, civil society movements, the media, and genuine\nanti-corruption initiatives.116 Some of his actions have undermined the government’s division of powers, verging\non illegality and unconstitutional action. These include his appointment of finance ministers before going through\nthe appropriate legislative procedure, weaponization of the judicial branch by threatening professors and scientists\nwith incarceration, organizing unlawful press conferences in support of his political party during the referendum\ncampaign, and using tax authority and never-ending audits to suppress corruption investigations.117\nOne of the most recent examples of blatant democratic backsliding is the electoral system reform AMLO\ncontinued to push for in an effort return to a time when the party in power controlled the electoral process.118\nAfter he abandoned his initial reform proposal due to lack of consensus in the Chamber of Deputies, AMLO\ndevised Plan B, which required only a simple majority for approval. This second proposal still reduced the\nsize of the organization, removed powers from the electoral arbiter limiting its ability to enforce electoral\nlaws, and softened sanctions for political parties. The plan was fast-tracked for approval in November 2022,\nundermining thorough analysis and discussion and circumventing the opposition almost entirely.119 The speed\nand coordination with which the reform came into being led many to conclude it had been AMLO’s plan all\nalong. On February 22, 2023, the plenary of the Senate approved the electoral reform without an eternal life\nclause, an article that allows the transfer of votes between parties forming a coalition in order to prevent\nthe disappearance of parties with little electoral strength, which struggled to find consensus.120 Indeed, the\nelectoral reform seems to have come as the most brazen of AMLO’s attacks against democracy, perhaps in\nan attempt to make the political context more favorable for the election of his preferred successor in 2024.\nThis has, in turn, provoked a forceful reaction among Mexican civil society, as there is unanimity among\nacademics, businesspeople, and political analysts that Mexican democracy rarely has sunk so low.121\nNot only did the electoral reform engender massive protests, but it also helped the opposition fully unify for\nthe first time since the beginning of AMLO’s mandate, bringing his approval rating to the lowest level since he\ncame to office.122 Popular pushback has been accompanied by resistance by some institutions remaining faithful\nto democratic principles. First among these is the Supreme Court. In April 2022 AMLO organized a referendum\non the president’s adequacy to remain in charge until the end of his mandate.123 Surprisingly, the court ruled\nin favor of the referendum regardless of the extremely low budget AMLO allocated to conducting it adequately.\nNevertheless, the Supreme Court demonstrated independence and eventually deemed the referendum invalid,\ndeclaring it undermined democracy by directly affecting citizens’ participation in the political process.124\nFurthermore, the opposition welcomed the appointment of Chief Justice Norma Lucía Piña Hernández in early\nJanuary 2023.125 As she did not belong to AMLO’s entourage, her election came as a positive sign for Mexican\ndemocracy. She is the first female chief justice and a supporter of the court’s independence and of human rights,\nespecially women’s rights.126 Together with other justices, many of whom have been criticized for siding with\nAMLO, she will review numerous pieces of legislation challenged by the opposition currently sitting before the\ncourt, including the decree extending the militarization of public security.127 Justice Alberto Pérez Dayán suspended\nthe first part of AMLO’s Plan B from entering into effect in the states where the electoral process had already\nbegun (Coahuila and State of Mexico). The court’s decision was subsequently validated as the INE organized two\nremarkably successful and transparent elections on June 4, demonstrating its continued trustworthiness and\nrelevance.128 The INE won further victories. In late June, the Supreme Court invalidated important parts of Plan B.\nIn response, AMLO promised to double down on Plan C, and promised to continue with his proposed constitutional\n23 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nreform that would allow the general public to elect judges and magistrates, in light of his belief that the judiciary\nrepresents opposition and conservative interests.\nMost recently, AMLO ordered senators to hold a session in a separate chamber to exclude the opposition. At\nthis unconstitutional session, the Senate passed 20 reforms in five hours on all manner of topics, including\nnew mining regulations that effectively shut down the industry in Mexico, ceding control and a majority of\nrevenues from the Maya Train and airports to the Mexican army. Senators also established a new tourism\nagency, 80 percent of whose fees would be allocated to the army. Many of the reforms, which included several\nconstitutional changes, run afoul of the USMCA. Such blatant violation of democratic procedure was evident in\npictures from the scene, which featured senators voting in the middle of the night on legislation they had not\nread, seated at plastic picnic tables without microphones.129\nTHE DEMOCRACY OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nWith the end of term of the INE’s 11 commissioners in 2023, AMLO will likely want to appoint more members\nloyal to him to further reduce the independence of Mexico’s electoral system.130 Some analysts fear that if the\nappointment is brought to a successful conclusion, together with the reform of the electoral process, Mexico’s\ndemocratic institutions will be further weakened, and the nation might not have free and fair elections in 2024.\nAMLO is dedicated to ensuring his preferred candidate is elected and continues his political agenda. In fact, he\nhas even entertained ideas of breaking the cardinal rule in Mexican politics and standing for a second term.131\nResistance by the opposition and the population plays a pivotal role in checking AMLO’s extension of power\nand his impact on democratic institutions. An even more crucial position is that of the Supreme Court, called\nto officially demonstrate its independence by voting on the constitutionality of Plan B, which is expected to\ntake place by June. This will continue to be key, especially as the 2024 electoral campaign gets closer and the\nMexican social, political, and economic context becomes more fragile.\nFigure 8: Mexico’s Democracy Index Score over Time\n8\nFlawed Democracy\n7\n6\n5\nHybrid Regime\n4\n2006 2008 2010 2012 2014 2016 2018 2020 2022\nSource: Economist Intelligence Unit (EIU), Democracy Index 2022 (EIU, 2022), https://www.eiu.com/n/campaigns/democracy-index-\n2022/#:~:text=Indicators,political%20participation%20and%20political%20culture.\nOn May 18, 2023, the Supreme Court denied AMLO’s claim that the permitting process for pet projects like the\nMaya Train could be expedited because they represented matters of “national security.” Mere hours after the\nverdict was handed down, a near-identical order was announced, redesignating the Maya Train, along with\n24 | Ryan C.
